People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m “not thinking straight,” especially my husband, but I’m telling you, something is going on. At the warehouse, the people there have been watching what I do, like watching my moves, where I go, what pallets I’m on, who I talk to. And then the pallet counts keep changing, but not like normal mistakes—more like they’re making it look like I messed up.

I was trying for that supervisor spot, and ever since that came up, things started feeling different. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.

Like I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.

My husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.

Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.

Lately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.

And at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.

experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.

At work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.

My lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.

Like I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.

So… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.

So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.

Like I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.

It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.

The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"When I try to explain what is going on, my words run from the forklift sheets to church signs to my husband's tone, and people tell me they can't follow what I'm saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.

I’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.

And I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m “not thinking straight,” especially my husband, but I’m telling you, something is going on. At the warehouse, the people there have been watching what I do, like watching my moves, where I go, what pallets I’m on, who I talk to. And then the pallet counts keep changing, but not like normal mistakes—more like they’re making it look like I messed up.\n\nI was trying for that supervisor spot, and ever since that came up, things started feeling different. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.

Like if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.

And to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.

I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / laborer_or_freight_stock_or_material_mover / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes when I'm in the aisle or folding clothes at home, I hear a man's voice clear as day saying, \"Nicole, check it again,\" even when nobody is near me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.

Like if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.

It happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.

---------- turn 11 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F23, F25.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m “not thinking straight,” especially my husband, but I’m telling you, something is going on. At the warehouse, the people there have been watching what I do, like watching my moves, where I go, what pallets I’m on, who I talk to. And then the pallet counts keep changing, but not like normal mistakes—more like they’re making it look like I messed up.\n\nI was trying for that supervisor spot, and ever since that came up, things started feeling different. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.

At work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.

My lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.

I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.

Like I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. In my mind it feels connected somehow, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out of my mouth people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying.

So… yes, they feel like my thoughts, but they don’t always come out in a way that makes sense to other people.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F23, F25.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m “not thinking straight,” especially my husband, but I’m telling you, something is going on. At the warehouse, the people there have been watching what I do, like watching my moves, where I go, what pallets I’m on, who I talk to. And then the pallet counts keep changing, but not like normal mistakes—more like they’re making it look like I messed up.\n\nI was trying for that supervisor spot, and ever since that came up, things started feeling different. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow.
