I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. Yeah… I have noticed it. It’s hard to explain because it’s not just “happy” or “mad,” it’s like some days I wake up and I feel really fired up, like I’ve got this push in me and nobody can slow me down. Like I’m moving around the house thinking, okay, I can handle everything today, I can get all this done, I don’t need anybody telling me how to do it.

But then it flips really fast if my husband says even one small thing. Like if he asks me about the kids, or what time I’m working, or what the plan is for the day, I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to control me or watch me. And sometimes after I say it, I know it came out too sharp, but in that second it feels like he’s getting in my way or questioning me, even if he’s probably just asking a normal question.

So yeah, my mood has been… more intense, I guess. Some days I feel like I’m running hot, like I’m ready to take on everything, and then I get irritated so fast over little things, especially at home.

It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.\n\nLike I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. In my mind it feels connected somehow, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out of my mouth people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nSo… yes, they feel like my thoughts, but they don’t always come out in a way that makes sense to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. It’s hard to explain because it’s not just “happy” or “mad,” it’s like some days I wake up and I feel really fired up, like I’ve got this push in me and nobody can slow me down. Like I’m moving around the house thinking, okay, I can handle everything today, I can get all this done, I don’t need anybody telling me how to do it.\n\nBut then it flips really fast if my husband says even one small thing. Like if he asks me about the kids, or what time I’m working, or what the plan is for the day, I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to control me or watch me. And sometimes after I say it, I know it came out too sharp, but in that second it feels like he’s getting in my way or questioning me, even if he’s probably just asking a normal question.\n\nSo yeah, my mood has been… more intense, I guess. Some days I feel like I’m running hot, like I’m ready to take on everything, and then I get irritated so fast over little things, especially at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it does make it hard, because I’ll be trying to do one thing and then when I try to explain it, or even just sort it out in my head, it gets all tangled.

Like with the forklift sheets — I’ll start there because that’s what I’m supposed to be thinking about, right? The sheets, the order, what needs to get moved. But then somehow my mind jumps to this church sign I saw, because the words on it stuck with me, and then that makes me think about something my husband said, or the way his tone sounded, and then I’m talking about that instead. And people are looking at me like, “What are you talking about?” or they’ll say they can’t follow me.

So it’s not like I’m trying to be confusing. In my head, it feels like it all connects, like one thing leads into the next. But when I say it out loud, it comes out all over the place, and then I lose track of what I was supposed to be deciding or finishing. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think I’m explaining myself clearly, and then I realize I’ve gone from work stuff to a sign to my husband’s tone and nobody knows how I got there.

But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.\n\nLike I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. In my mind it feels connected somehow, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out of my mouth people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nSo… yes, they feel like my thoughts, but they don’t always come out in a way that makes sense to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. It’s hard to explain because it’s not just “happy” or “mad,” it’s like some days I wake up and I feel really fired up, like I’ve got this push in me and nobody can slow me down. Like I’m moving around the house thinking, okay, I can handle everything today, I can get all this done, I don’t need anybody telling me how to do it.\n\nBut then it flips really fast if my husband says even one small thing. Like if he asks me about the kids, or what time I’m working, or what the plan is for the day, I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to control me or watch me. And sometimes after I say it, I know it came out too sharp, but in that second it feels like he’s getting in my way or questioning me, even if he’s probably just asking a normal question.\n\nSo yeah, my mood has been… more intense, I guess. Some days I feel like I’m running hot, like I’m ready to take on everything, and then I get irritated so fast over little things, especially at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to make decisions or complete tasks in your daily life due to your thoughts feeling disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does make it hard, because I’ll be trying to do one thing and then when I try to explain it, or even just sort it out in my head, it gets all tangled.\n\nLike with the forklift sheets — I’ll start there because that’s what I’m supposed to be thinking about, right? The sheets, the order, what needs to get moved. But then somehow my mind jumps to this church sign I saw, because the words on it stuck with me, and then that makes me think about something my husband said, or the way his tone sounded, and then I’m talking about that instead. And people are looking at me like, “What are you talking about?” or they’ll say they can’t follow me.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m trying to be confusing. In my head, it feels like it all connects, like one thing leads into the next. But when I say it out loud, it comes out all over the place, and then I lose track of what I was supposed to be deciding or finishing. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think I’m explaining myself clearly, and then I realize I’ve gone from work stuff to a sign to my husband’s tone and nobody knows how I got there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t always know how to put a line around it and say, “this was the exact period,” but there have definitely been stretches where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.

That hurts to hear, because I don’t feel like I’m trying to be unreliable. I’ll think I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into me not following through or things not getting handled right. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because everybody keeps saying I’m “not thinking straight,” especially my husband, but I’m telling you, something is going on. At the warehouse, the people there have been watching what I do, like watching my moves, where I go, what pallets I’m on, who I talk to. And then the pallet counts keep changing, but not like normal mistakes—more like they’re making it look like I messed up.\n\nI was trying for that supervisor spot, and ever since that came up, things started feeling different. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.\n\nLike I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. The sheets, the order, what needs to get moved. In my head, it feels like it all connects, like one thing leads into the next. But when I say it out loud, it comes out all over the place, and then I lose track of what I was supposed to be deciding or finishing. It’s frustrating, because I’ll think I’m explaining myself clearly, and then I realize I’ve gone from work stuff to a sign to my husband’s tone and nobody knows how I got there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where your thoughts, feelings, and behaviors were so abnormal that they interfered with your daily life for an extended period, say a few days or weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. And I could tell people were getting frustrated, because in that kind of job, if I’m taking too long or rechecking things or getting mixed up, it affects everybody around me.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said he can’t rely on me the way he used to. That hurts to hear, because I don’t feel like I’m trying to be unreliable. I’ll think I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into me not following through or things not getting handled right. So yes, it has interfered — not just for a day where I’m having a bad mood, but enough that work changed what they let me do, and at home it’s caused problems too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I can say for sure like, “yes, exactly two weeks.” I’ve had days where I felt really low or just didn’t want to deal with anything, but when you say two weeks straight, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy, I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s kind of hard for me to separate it out, because sometimes I’m not doing things because I’m tired, or because there’s too much going on, or because I’m irritated and don’t want to be bothered. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think I have, but it’s hard for me to explain because I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m sad and that’s why I’m not doing it.” It’s more like I just… don’t really get pulled toward things the way I used to.

At home, a lot of the time I just sit there. I’ll have the TV on, or I’ll be scrolling on my phone, but I’m not really into it. It’s just something to look at. My husband will try to joke with me or get me to laugh, and I can hear him, I know he’s trying, but it’s like nothing really shows up on my face. I don’t mean to be cold to him. I’ll think in my head, “That was supposed to be funny,” or “I should smile,” but it doesn’t come out right.

And then he’ll ask me what’s wrong, or he’ll say I’m just staring, and I don’t always have an answer. I’m not trying to ignore him. I just feel kind of blank sitting there, like I’m present but not really reacting much. So yeah, I guess I don’t enjoy things the same, or at least I don’t show it like I used to.

Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.\n\nLike I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. Like suddenly my husband is acting like I’m crazy when I try to explain it, and the people at work are acting innocent, but I can see how they’re setting it up. They change numbers around or make it seem like I didn’t do something right, and then if I get upset about it, that just makes me look worse. So I came in because everyone keeps putting it on me, but I need somebody to understand that I’m not just making this up.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, sometimes. Not all the time, but enough that it bothers me. Like if I’m in the aisle at work, and there’s nobody close enough to be talking to me, I’ll hear a man’s voice really clear, like right near me, saying, Nicole, check it again. And it’s not like a thought in my head, it sounds like somebody actually said it.\n\nIt’s happened at home too, like when I’m folding clothes and the house is quiet, and I’ll hear it again, that same kind of voice telling me to check it again. I’ll stop and look around because it sounds that real, but nobody’s there. It makes me nervous because I don’t know where it’s coming from.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these voices and feeling like you're being watched?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about eight months now, I think. Around eight months is when I started really noticing it wasn’t just little things or me being tired. The feeling like people were watching me at work, keeping track of what I was doing, and then the counts or things being changed around—that’s been going on for about that long.\n\nThe voices too, maybe not every single day, but that same stretch of time. It kind of all started around the same period, and then it just kept happening, enough that I couldn’t just brush it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to express your thoughts or feel like your thoughts are disjointed when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people say that to me a lot, that they can’t follow what I’m saying. I don’t always notice it at first, because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to explain what’s happening at work, and I’ll start with the forklift sheets because that’s where the numbers are, then I’ll remember a sign I saw outside church that said something about truth or being tested, and then I’ll think about the way my husband said my name that morning, like his tone was off, and to me those things all fit together somehow. But when I’m saying it out loud, people look at me like I jumped from one thing to another.\n\nMy husband will say, “Nicole, slow down, you’re not making sense,” and that frustrates me because I am trying to make sense. I’m trying to lay it all out, but then my words kind of run ahead of me, and I end up talking about three different parts at once. Then I get upset because I feel like if I could just explain it the right way, people would understand what I mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your daily routines or activities, like having trouble completing tasks or keeping track of things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s actually been one of the biggest problems. At work especially. I used to be able to just get into my routine, you know, do the counts, move things where they needed to go, keep the line going. I wasn’t perfect or anything, but I knew what I was doing.\n\nLately it’s like I get stuck checking and rechecking, or I lose where I was in the count, and then everything takes longer. My supervisors noticed. They took me off the forklift and some of the other safety stuff because they said I was slowing the line down and they couldn’t have mistakes there. That felt really bad, because I don’t want them thinking I’m lazy or careless. I’m trying, but I’ll get caught up making sure it’s right, and then I’m behind.\n\nAnd at home too, my husband says he can’t rely on me the way he used to. Like with regular things, getting something done when I say I will, keeping track of what needs to happen around the house. I’ll start something and then it doesn’t get finished right, or I take too long, and then he gets frustrated. I know it’s affecting things. I don’t like admitting that, but it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things that don't make sense to you or others, like acting on impulses without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, at the time it doesn’t always feel like I’m “acting weird,” it feels like I’m trying to get something settled before I can move on. But other people definitely don’t understand it.\n\nAt work I started doing this thing where I’d walk the same path around the pallets over and over before I could actually move anything. Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. I’d go around and then think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t check it the right way, and then I’d do it again. And the labels too — I’d line them up or straighten them so they were facing a certain way, even if that wasn’t what I was supposed to be focused on. My lead told me to stop, and I heard him, I really did, but it was like my body still needed to finish it before I could move on.\n\nSo… unusual, yes. Out of character, probably. I didn’t used to get stuck like that. It’s embarrassing because I know it looks like I’m ignoring people or being difficult, but that’s not what it feels like from inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or speaking in a way that seems disjointed when you're talking to friends or family, not just when discussing work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think that happens. My husband points it out more than anybody. I’ll try to explain what I mean, like why I got upset or what I was trying to say, and somehow I start in one place and end up somewhere totally different.\n\nLike I’ll be talking about the forklift sheets from work, because that’s where my mind starts, with the counts and the papers and whether I wrote something down right. Then suddenly I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, because the wording on it stuck in my head, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone of voice when he asked me something, because that reminded me of feeling like I was being corrected. And he’ll stop me and say, “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” or “You’re jumping around.” And I get frustrated because in my head it feels connected, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out loud I guess it doesn’t sound that way.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I feel like if I could just explain it from the beginning, they’d understand, but then the beginning keeps moving, if that makes sense. So with family or friends, yeah, sometimes they tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, and then I feel like I have to explain even more, which probably makes it worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble organizing your thoughts or activities when you're not at work, like when you're doing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at home too, but I notice it more when I’m trying to explain something than when I’m, like, folding clothes or whatever. It’s like my thoughts won’t stay in a straight line long enough for me to get the thing out.\n\nI’ll try to tell my husband what’s going on with me, and I start with something simple, like the forklift sheets from work — because that’s what I’m thinking about, whether I wrote something down right or whether the counts matched. But then while I’m talking, my mind catches on something else, like a church sign I saw, because the words on it felt important or just stuck there in my head. And then somehow I’m talking about his tone of voice, like the way he asked me a question, because that made me feel like I was being corrected.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like three different conversations. He tells me that too. He’ll say, “Nicole, I can’t follow you,” or “What are you talking about?” But inside my head it feels connected. It’s just when I say it out loud, it comes out all tangled. Then I try to back up and explain it better, but backing up makes me add more details, and then I can tell he’s even more lost. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be confusing. I’m trying really hard to make it make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having thoughts that feel disconnected or hard to follow when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. It’s not only work, even though work is where the examples come from a lot because that stuff gets stuck in my head first.\n\nLike if I’m just sitting at home and trying to explain what’s going on with me, my thoughts don’t stay in order. I’ll start with the forklift sheets, because I’m thinking about whether I wrote something wrong or if the numbers matched, and then before I even finish that thought I’m talking about a church sign I saw, because some words on it stuck with me, and then somehow I’m on my husband’s tone of voice, like how he asked me something and how it made me feel corrected.\n\nAnd to me, while I’m saying it, it feels like there’s a path between all those things. Like one thought grabs the next one. But other people don’t hear the path, I guess. My husband will say, “I can’t follow what you’re saying,” and then I get upset because I feel like I am explaining it, but the more I try to make it clear, the more I add, and then it gets even harder to follow. So yes, the thoughts feel disconnected sometimes, or at least they come out that way.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices you hear ever tell you to do something specific or influence your thoughts about what's happening at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like they tell me to do something big or anything, but it’ll be very specific in the moment.\n\nLike if I’m in the aisle at work, just standing there with the sheets or looking at something I already checked, I’ll hear a man’s voice clear as day say, Nicole, check it again. And I’ll look around because it sounds like somebody is near me, like close enough that I should be able to see them, but there won’t be anybody there. It’s not like a thought exactly, because it doesn’t feel like my own voice in my head. It sounds like a man saying it to me.\n\nIt happens at home too, like when I’m folding clothes or doing something normal, and then I hear it again — Nicole, check it again. And then I do start wondering if I missed something, or if I messed something up, even if I was just doing laundry. It makes me feel on edge, because once I hear it, I can’t just ignore it easily. I start thinking maybe I should go back over whatever I was doing, even if nobody else would think anything was wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been engaging in any repetitive behaviors or rituals at home that are not part of your usual routine?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. I didn’t really think of it as a “ritual” at first, because it felt more like I was just trying to make things feel right before I could keep going.\n\nAt work, I started walking the same path around the pallets, like around and around the same way, before I would move anything. I’d tell myself I was just checking, making sure I wasn’t missing something, but then I’d do it again even after I already looked. And the labels too — I’d line them up a certain way, like they had to be straight or facing right before I could touch the pallet or move on to the next thing.\n\nMy lead noticed and told me to stop, like, “Nicole, just move it, you already checked it,” and I’d feel embarrassed because I knew I was taking too long. But if I tried to skip it, I’d get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I couldn’t start the next part yet. It wasn’t part of how I normally do my job. It just kind of started happening, and then it got hard to not do it.\n\nDoctor: When you have these thoughts that feel disconnected, do you feel like they are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like mine, yeah… like I’m not saying someone put them there or anything. It’s more that when I try to explain what’s going on, my own thoughts just start running together too fast or jumping around.\n\nLike I’ll start trying to tell somebody about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m worried about first, and then while I’m still talking I’ll remember a church sign I saw because the words stuck in my head, and then I’m suddenly talking about my husband’s tone and how he said something to me. In my mind it feels connected somehow, like one thing leads to the next, but when it comes out of my mouth people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying.\n\nSo… yes, they feel like my thoughts, but they don’t always come out in a way that makes sense to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed it. It’s hard to explain because it’s not just “happy” or “mad,” it’s like some days I wake up and I feel really fired up, like I’ve got this push in me and nobody can slow me down. Like I’m moving around the house thinking, okay, I can handle everything today, I can get all this done, I don’t need anybody telling me how to do it.\n\nBut then it flips really fast if my husband says even one small thing. Like if he asks me about the kids, or what time I’m working, or what the plan is for the day, I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to control me or watch me. And sometimes after I say it, I know it came out too sharp, but in that second it feels like he’s getting in my way or questioning me, even if he’s probably just asking a normal question.\n\nSo yeah, my mood has been… more intense, I guess. Some days I feel like I’m running hot, like I’m ready to take on everything, and then I get irritated so fast over little things, especially at home.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to make decisions or complete tasks in your daily life due to your thoughts feeling disorganized?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does make it hard, because I’ll be trying to do one thing and then when I try to explain it, or even just sort it out in my head, it gets all tangled.\n\nLike with the forklift sheets — I’ll start there because that’s what I’m supposed to be thinking about, right? That was embarrassing, honestly. They said my counts were getting off and my routines were slowing the line down, like I wasn’t keeping things moving the way I should. And I could tell people were getting frustrated, because in that kind of job, if I’m taking too long or rechecking things or getting mixed up, it affects everybody around me.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said he can’t rely on me the way he used to. That hurts to hear, because I don’t feel like I’m trying to be unreliable. I’ll think I’m doing what I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into me not following through or things not getting handled right. So yes, it has interfered — not just for a day where I’m having a bad mood, but enough that work changed what they let me do, and at home it’s caused problems too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt depressed or lost interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I can say for sure like, “yes, exactly two weeks.” I’ve had days where I felt really low or just didn’t want to deal with anything, but when you say two weeks straight, or losing interest in things I normally enjoy, I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s kind of hard for me to separate it out, because sometimes I’m not doing things because I’m tired, or because there’s too much going on, or because I’m irritated and don’t want to be bothered. But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed for that long.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t noticed a clear stretch like that, not one I can point to and say, “that was two weeks of depression.” I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have, but it’s hard for me to explain because I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m sad and that’s why I’m not doing it.” It’s more like I just… don’t really get pulled toward things the way I used to.\n\nAt home, a lot of the time I just sit there. Yes… I think so. It’s not only when I’m nervous or upset. Even when I’m just trying to explain something normal, like what happened at work, it comes out all tangled.

Like I’ll start talking about the forklift sheets, because that’s what I’m trying to explain, and then somehow I’m talking about a sign I saw outside a church, and then I’m talking about my husband’s tone from earlier that morning. In my head it feels connected while I’m saying it, like one thing leads to the next, but then people look at me like I skipped a bunch of steps.

Or at work, if I’m trying to explain why I did something a certain way, I can tell they’re not following me. They’ll kind of stop me and ask one question at a time, like they have to pull it out of me in pieces.

So yeah, my thoughts don’t always stay in a straight line, even if I’m not feeling stressed right that second. It’s embarrassing, because I’m trying to make sense, but my words go all over the place.

Like I’d go around one side, then the other, and if it didn’t feel right, I’d have to do it again. And then with the labels, I’d stop and line them up a certain way, like straightening them or making sure they were facing right, even when that wasn’t really part of what I was supposed to be doing right then.\n\nMy lead told me to stop more than once. He’d be like, “Nicole, just move it, we don’t have time for that,” and I knew he was getting irritated, but I still felt stuck doing it. It’s embarrassing because I know from the outside it probably looks pointless, like I’m just wasting time or not listening. But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. But it’s not like I decide, “I’m going to act strange now.” It feels more like I get caught in a pattern and I can’t just drop it.\n\nLike I was saying, with the pallets, I’d walk the same path around them more than once before I could actually move anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “extremely happy” exactly, but there are definitely days where I feel really fired up, like I’m moving fast inside and nobody can slow me down. Like I have this strong feeling of, “I know what I’m doing, just let me do it,” and everything feels like it needs to happen right now.

But it can flip into irritation really quick. Like if my husband asks me one small question — something normal, like about the kids, or what time I’m going somewhere, or what my schedule is — I’ll snap at him like he’s trying to control me. And afterward I can kind of see that maybe he was just asking, but in the moment it feels like he’s blocking me or checking up on me, and I get mad fast.

My thoughts do feel like they’re going fast during that, too. Like I’m already three steps ahead in my head, and then someone asks me something simple and it feels like they’re interrupting the whole thing. So yes, I’ve had periods like that — more fired up and irritable than just happy, I guess.

But in the moment it’s like I can’t go to the next thing until it’s done the way it needs to be done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been doing things in a way that seems unusual or out of character for you, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, mostly I notice it at work because that’s where people are watching me more, and where there’s a pace I’m supposed to keep. I mean, I don’t always know how to put a line around it and say, “this was the exact period,” but there have definitely been stretches where I wasn’t functioning like I normally do.\n\nAt work it got bad enough that my supervisors took me off the forklift and other stuff where you really have to be sharp, safety-wise. That was embarrassing, honestly. They said my counts were getting off and my routines were slowing the line down, like I wasn’t keeping things moving the way I should. And I could tell people were getting frustrated, because in that kind of job, if I’m taking too long or rechecking things or getting mixed up, it affects everybody around me.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has said he can’t rely on me the way he used to. That hurts to hear, because I don’t feel like I’m trying to be unreliable. The symptoms do not meet the full criteria for paranoid schizophrenia (F20.0) or schizoaffective disorder (F25.0), which would require a longer duration of symptoms or a more prominent mood component.",
  "diagnostic_checklist": {
